1) N–B → ... → M
2) N–C → ... → M
3) N–O → ... → M

- O connects to P and C; P connects to J and I; J connects to M. So N–O–P–J–M is a path.

Thus all N–M paths start with one of the three edges (N,B), (N,C), or (N,O). Removing all three isolates N from the rest of the network that reaches M. Removing only one or two leaves a remaining route via another neighbor, so three edges are necessary and sufficient.

   Observation: All these routes enter the “right side” via either J or P or directly into H. But every path from E to I that goes upward passes through at least one of {H, J, P} right before reaching I (edges present: H–I, J–I, P–I).

2) Through the lower part:
   - E–B–C–O–P–I
   - E–N–O–P–I
   - E–N–Y–O–P–I
   - E–B–C–H–I (via C–H)
   - E–N–C–H–I, etc.
   Observation: All lower routes reach I via either H or P (since O connects to P and C connects to H; there is no direct O–I or C–I edge).

- Therefore, every E→I path must pass through at least one of the three neighbors of I: H, J, or P.

- Check minimality:
  - Removing a single node among {H, J, P} is insufficient: for example, if H is removed, E–M–J–I still connects; if J is removed, E–B–C–H–I still connects; if P is removed, E–M–J–I or E–B–C–H–I still connects.
  - Removing any two of them disconnects E and I because I’s only remaining neighbor would be the third, and there is always an E→that neighbor path; but with two removed, I has at most one neighbor left. We must ensure no E→I path via the remaining neighbor:
    • Remove H and J: remaining neighbor P; E can reach P (e.g., via O or J? J removed; via O: E–B–C–O–P). But I is connected to P via edge P–I; however P is not removed. Wait—since H and J are gone, the only edge into I is P–I, so E→P→I still exists. Hence we must include P in the cut if H and J are both present? Correction: to fully disconnect, we must remove all neighbors of I that are reachable from E. E can reach all three H, J, P. Thus we need to remove at least two neighbors so that none of the remaining neighbors is available.
    Test pairs:
    • Remove H and P: I’s only neighbor left is J. Can E reach J? Yes via E–M–J or E–G–…–J. Hence path remains. Not sufficient.
    • Remove J and P: I’s only neighbor left is H. Can E reach H? Yes via E–G–H or E–C–H. Path remains. Not sufficient.
    • Remove H and J: remaining neighbor P; E reaches P via E–B–C–O–P or E–N–O–P. Path remains. Not sufficient.

  Thus, removing any two of {H, J, P} is not enough. We must remove all three neighbors of I.

- Verify sufficiency of removing {H, J, P}:
  - With H, J, P removed, node I has no remaining incident edges. Therefore, no E→I path can exist regardless of the rest of the graph.

- Verify minimality:
  - A cut of size 2 fails as shown above. Therefore, the minimum cut size is 3.

Final Answer:

Step-by-step reasoning:
- Target nodes are B and H. There is a direct edge (B, H).
- If we remove (B, H), we must check for alternative paths from B to H.

Explore neighbors of B:
- B connects to N, C (via rectangle path), and E (vertical edge), besides H.

Paths via N/C side toward H:
- From B→N→Y/O→P→I→(outer frame)→H exists because I connects to the outer frame which goes to H.
- From B→C→I→(outer frame)→H also exists.

Paths via E/F/K/G side:
- B→E→F→X→K→G→H is another route.
- B→E→L→(top frame)→F→...→H also leads to H through the same K/G branch.

Thus, to disconnect, we must block access from B to both “left-bottom outer frame” (through C/N→I/P) and to the “right branch to G” (through E/F/K/X).

Identify minimal cut:
- Cut 1: remove (B, E) to stop all routes from B going to the right branch and top. Without (B, E), B cannot reach F/K/G side.
- Remaining routes go left/down: B→N or B→C.

To block those, it suffices to sever B’s connection to that left/down cluster at a single edge because both N and C connect to the same region through I/P.
- Remove (B, N) OR (B, C). If we remove only one, the other still provides a path to I→outer→H. So we must remove both (B, N) and (B, C) or alternatively cut later at (I, outer) which isn’t a single edge.
- Therefore, remove (B, N) and (B, C).

Verification:
- After removing [(B, E), (B, N), (B, C)], B has no remaining path to H: the direct edge (B, H) still needs consideration. We must also remove (B, H) since it directly connects them.
- With (B, H) removed plus the three cuts above, there is no connection from B to the right branch (cut at B, E) and no connection to the left-bottom cluster (cuts at B, N and B, C). Hence all paths are broken.

Minimality:
- At least one edge must remove the direct link (B, H).
- After removing (B, H), there remain two disjoint groups of paths: via E-right and via N/C-left. Each requires at least one cut. The minimal way is to cut them at B’s incident edges, needing two more edges, totaling three. But because B connects to both N and C to reach the left cluster, we need both removed, so total becomes four. No set of three edges can block both left entrances (N and C) and the right entrance (E) and the direct edge simultaneously.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(B, H), (B, E), (B, N), (B, C)]

1) E → F → X → K → (up square) back to K only; the only forward way from this cluster to the right is via G or H:
   - E → F → X → G → H → I → (up to J via I–P–J or I–P–D–(loop)–P–J, etc.)
   - E → F → X → G → H → B (down) → C → P → J (via C–I–P–J or C–O–P–J)

5) E → B → C → N → (up to C/O only) then O → P → J
6) E → M → (left frame to top) → across to J: The outer frame from M goes up to the top border and across directly to J. Therefore there exists a path E → M → (outer frame) → J.

- The single vertex M lies on the only connection from E to the outer frame. However, E also has internal routes to J through the central band B/C/H/I/O/P. Thus removing M alone does not disconnect E and J.
- To block the internal band, note every E→J path not using M must pass through one of the two nodes B or F on leaving E:
   - From E to the right: E→B
   - From E up into the K–X–G–H area: E→F

- Paths via the center/right (E→B … →J) are cut.
- Paths via the upper-left cluster (E→F→X/G/H … →J) are cut.
- The only remaining neighbor is M, giving path E→M→(outer frame)→J. So we must also block M or some vertex on that frame before reaching J.

Thus a cut set {M, B} or {M, F} might work. Check minimality:
- Remove {M, B}: 
  - E→F→…→H→I→P→J still exists (via F). So not disconnected.
- Remove {M, F}:
  - E→B→…→J still exists (via B). So not disconnected.
- Remove {M, B, F}: 
  - All routes from E are eliminated: E’s only neighbors removed; no path can leave E toward J.
- Could a 2-node cut exist elsewhere (e.g., cutting I and P)? Even if we cut I and P, E could still reach J via outer frame through M, so M must be in any cut. With M removed, can a single additional node suffice? If we remove M plus one node:
  - If with M and B removed, E→F→…→J still exists.
  - If with M and F removed, E→B→…→J still exists.
  Therefore at least three nodes are required, specifically M and both B and F.

Conclusion: Minimum cut is 3 nodes: M, B, and F.

Yes: B–N edge exists, also C–N, and Y–N; so both N and B are in the same component with H. Therefore, to block all interior-to-E paths, we must cut both (E,B) and (E,N) unless there is some earlier single edge separating H from both B and N. But H is directly connected to B, so any cut not involving E would need to break H–B, but then paths via H–I–…–C–N–E might still reach N and then E. Is there a path from H to N not using H–B? - (E,F)
- (E,M)
- (E,B)
- (E,N)

  - If (E,F) kept, H–G–…–F–E connects.
  - If (E,M) kept, H–I–P–J–M–E connects.

Hence each is necessary. Could a smaller cut exist by removing edges earlier to block multiple channels at once? But are there any other paths from H to E not using H’s three incident edges? No, any path must start by leaving H via one of its incident edges. Therefore removing all three edges incident to H is a cut set of size 3, and it is minimal? Could we do it with only 2 edges? If we cut (H,I) and (H,B), path via G still exists to F–E. If we cut (H,I) and (H,G), path via B remains. If we cut (H,B) and (H,G), path via I–…–M–E remains. So 2 is insufficient. Therefore minimum is 3.

Choose the 3-edge cut at H as it’s smaller than cutting 4 at E.

- Any other neighbor to I? There is also a diagonal edge I–C which is removed; horizontal to H removed.

With H and C removed and P removed, J remains but J–I path exists, so G can reach J and then I directly, so fails. {J, C, P}: H path exists. {H, J, P}: C path exists. - From L, any path must go through either E or M. So any L–J path begins L→E→… or L→M→….

- M connects to E and J (via edge M–J) and L.

  1) E–P–J (since P connects directly to J).
  2) E–P–D–J (P–D edge exists).
  3) E–B–I–J (B–I edge exists and I–J edge exists).

Therefore, to block E→J, it suffices to remove either P or I or B in combinations that kill all alternatives. Observe:
- If we remove P, paths 1,2,4 (and any via F because F routes go through P) are gone, but E–B–I–J (path 3) still works unless we also hit B or I.
- If we remove I, paths 3 and 4 are gone, but E–P–J and E–P–D–J remain unless we also hit P.
- If we remove B, path 3 is gone, but paths via P remain; also E could go E–B–… elsewhere to I? Without B, E doesn’t reach I except via P (E–P–I), so again P remains the key.

- Remove M and I: With I removed, paths 3 and 4 are gone. Remaining possibilities via P: E–P–J and E–P–D–J both require P, which is still present, so they still work. Therefore M+I alone is NOT sufficient.

- With M, P, and I removed, from L the only branch is via E; from E:
  - Direct to J via P is gone.
  - Via D requires P (E–P–D–J), blocked.
  - Via B–I–J is blocked (I removed).
  - Any other route from E to J goes through P (toward F then to P then J), blocked.
Therefore all E→J paths are gone, hence all L→J paths are blocked.

Try M and B only: E–P–I–J remains. M and P only: E–B–I–J remains. M and I only: E–P–J remains. Hence any 2-node set including M fails.

Is there a 2-node cut not including M? If M remains, L–M–J gives a direct path, so impossible.

But is three still minimal? Yes, as argued, any 2-node cut fails.

- Remaining possibility to J is via right side through C/I. Keep (B,C) intact; then B→C→I→J leads to M via (J,M). So to complete cut we also need to sever that, but we already used only two edges: (H,G) and (B,E). Still a path exists. If instead of (B,E), cut (J,M). Try cut set: (H,G) and (J,M).
Check all paths:
- To reach M via J: blocked by (J,M).
- To reach M via F/E/L: need either B→E direct or B→H→G→F→... Since (H,G) is cut, the second route is gone, but B can still go directly to E via (B,E) then E→M or E→F→M or E→L→M. So we also need to cut (B,E). That’s again 3.

Try cut set: (B,C) and (B,N). Then B cannot go right to C/N; only exits are (B,E) and (B,H). From there to M via E/F. Are there disjoint two routes? B→E→M and B→H→G→F→M. These both funnel into M through (F,M) or (E,M)/(L,M). If we cut a single edge (F,M), the E branch remains; with (E,M) cut, the H→G→F→M via (F,M) remains. So need at least 2 more. Not good.

Consider a 2-edge cut: remove (E,F) and (J,K)? But E→M still exists; F→M still exists via G path from B. Hard.

Conclusion: Minimum is 3 edges by cutting all entries into M: (E,M), (F,M), (J,M). Verify no 2-edge cut exists: Because there are three internally disjoint B–M paths:
- Path 1: B–E–M.
- Path 2: B–H–G–F–M. (Edges disjoint from path1 except at M)
- Path 3: B–C–I–J–M or B–N–C–I–J–M. This is edge-disjoint from paths 1 and 2 until M.
Since there are three edge-disjoint paths, by Menger’s theorem, the edge-connectivity between B and M is at least 3; hence a cut needs at least 3 edges. The set {(E,M), (F,M), (J,M)} achieves 3, so it is minimum.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, M), (F, M), (J, M)].

Crucially, every H–E path uses one of the two edges incident to E: (E, B) or (E, P). There is no alternative entry to E from the rest of the graph.

Minimality verification:
- Removing only one edge (E, B) leaves H–I–P–E.
- Removing only (E, P) leaves H–C–B–E (or H–I–B–E).
- Removing both guarantees no path from the network to E except L/M which are dead ends.

Hence the minimum cut is 2 edges.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(E, B), (E, P)]

- List B’s connections: B–N, B–C, B–H (direct), and B–E? (there is a line from B up-left to E along the outer edge passing through N; it is continuous only via nodes, so direct edges from B are: (B,N), (B,C), (B,H).)
- H’s connections: H–B, H–G, H–I (diagonal to the right), and H–E? The slanted line from H to E does not touch E; only lines from H go to B, G, and I.

Paths from B to H:
1) Direct edge (B, H).
2) Via right side: B–C–I–H, and B–C–J–…–I–H (since J also connects to I), and B–C–P–I–H through P (P connects to I and J and O, and to D; but all still funnel into I before H).
3) Via left/upper side: B–N–… does N connect to H? N connects to C and O and Y, but none go to H except through C/I. So any path via N must pass through C then I to reach H.
4) Via E side: B is not directly connected to E; the only way from B toward E is B–N–Y–O–P–…–I–H or B–C–I–… All still pass through edge into I before H.

Thus, every B–H path uses either:
- the direct edge (B, H), or
- the edge (I, H) as the final step from the right/outer side.

Therefore, removing edges (B, H) and (I, H) disconnects B from H. After removing both:
- Any routes through C, J, P, N, O, etc., reach I but cannot go from I to H.
- Any local route from G or E cannot reach B without using H–B (since G connects to H, F/X/K/E area but those don’t connect to B except back through H or through I-side which still needs (I,H)). With (I,H) removed, that is blocked; with (B,H) removed, the direct link is gone.

No single edge removal suffices:
- Removing only (B, H) leaves B–C–I–H.
- Removing only (I, H) leaves the direct edge (B, H).

Hence the minimal cut size is 2.

Final Answer:

So the set must include either (J, D) or block reaching D by removing (P, D). However, we are limited to 2 edges; seems impossible.

Conclusion: At least three edges are necessary. One valid minimal cut is to remove all three non-D entrances and also block D, but with only three we must choose wisely:
- Remove (J, I), (J, M), and (J, P). Then the only remaining entrance to J is (J, D). From F, the only way to reach D is through P–D, but P is disconnected from J; yet reaching D is still possible via P–D, but that doesn’t connect to J unless (J, D) exists. But D connects to J directly; even if we reach D, the last hop (D, J) would connect. Therefore we must instead remove (J, D) in place of one of the others and ensure there’s no remaining route:
Choose cut set: (J, I), (J, M), (J, D). With these gone, any path must use (J, P). Can F reach P? Yes via …→O→P or …→I→P. That would still connect to J via (J, P), so this fails unless we remove J–P instead.

Thus the only way is to remove all four edges incident to J, or remove three edges where the remaining one is unreachable from F. The only candidate for being unreachable is D–J if D is unreachable from F after removing some other edge. But D is reachable from F via …→O→P→D. To block that with one more removal, we’d need to cut (P, D) instead of (J, D). Then with removals (J, I), (J, M), (P, D), the only remaining entrance to J is (J, P). F can still reach P via O–P or I–P, so this still connects.

Therefore, to ensure disconnection, we must remove at least 4 edges: all edges incident to J.

Final Answer:
